========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a memory of it going away in some clear, complete way and then later coming back, like a switch turning off and on again.

I think there have been times where I was doing better, or where I was busy enough that I wasn’t focused on it, or I could function and tell myself things were okay. But when you say “completely gone away,” I get stuck, because I’m not sure I can identify that. It’s possible I just didn’t notice it as much, or I explained it away as stress or being tired.

So… I wouldn’t want to say yes, because that feels too certain. What I can say is I’ve had periods that felt less bad than others, but I can’t really point to a time where I’m confident the depressive symptoms were totally gone for a while.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I got scared of my own thoughts. It’s hard to say out loud, because part of me feels dramatic saying it, but I keep having these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not that I have some big speech prepared or anything, it’s more like this quiet thought that shows up when I’m exhausted.\n\nAnd sometimes, when I’m driving home from the clinic after a long day, I’ll catch myself thinking… what if I just didn’t turn the wheel? Like, what would happen if I just let the car keep going. And then I get frightened because I don’t want to be the kind of person who thinks that, and I don’t want to scare anyone, but it’s been happening enough that I felt like I shouldn’t ignore it anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel while driving?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I think. Not years or anything like that, at least not in this intense way. It really started around this whole crisis with taking in our relative, and since then everything has felt so up and down and overwhelming.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself it’s just stress and that once things settle down I’ll be fine, but for about a month now it’s been like my mind goes to those places when I’m worn out. It’s not every second, but it’s been long enough and intense enough that I finally thought, okay, this isn’t just a bad day anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing, like have you been feeling less interested in hobbies or activities that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. That’s one of the things that scares me, because those things are usually how I know I’m still… me.\n\nI have a camera I used to carry around all the time, even just in the yard or on walks, and I haven’t touched it. Not even picked it up to check the battery or look through old photos. And the bird feeders — normally I’m the person who notices who’s coming in, what birds are out there, whether the feeders need filling, all of that. Lately I’ll look out the window and it’s like I’m seeing it but not really caring. I might think, oh, I should refill that, and then I just don’t.\n\nSame with the garden. Usually I’m thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs pruning, what might bloom soon, and right now I just… don’t care. It feels awful to say that, because those are not just hobbies to me, they’re the things that used to make me feel grounded and like myself. So yes, I’ve definitely felt less interested, and it’s frightening how flat it all feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… constantly. And it’s not always over something that, rationally, I know should carry that much weight, but in my head it becomes this whole indictment of who I am.\n\nLike with everything that’s changed at home, I keep catching myself feeling resentful about the disruption, and then immediately I’m ashamed of that. I’ll think, what kind of person resents this? What kind of person gets irritated when people need help? And then I just spiral into telling myself I’m terrible, selfish, cold… all of it.\n\nAnd I replay things, too. If I had an impatient look on my face, or if my voice came out sharper than I meant it to, I’ll go over it again and again later. I’ll think, see, that’s who you really are. That proves it. My husband and the family would be better off without having to deal with me and whatever is wrong with me.\n\nI know if someone else told me the same story, I probably wouldn’t judge them that harshly. But when it’s me, it feels like evidence. Like every little thing I do wrong confirms I’m just failing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yes. My energy feels like it’s just… gone. I don’t know how else to say it. Everything takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, even just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s not a long distance, and normally I wouldn’t even think about it — I’d just be moving from one thing to the next — but now I notice every step. I feel heavy, like my body is resisting me.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m completely spent. I can barely make dinner, and even helping with the evening care routine feels like this huge mountain I have to climb. I’m still doing things because they need to be done, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been really bad. I used to be able to sleep and at least feel like I’d had some kind of reset, even if I was tired, but now it doesn’t feel like sleep is doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nA lot of nights I just lie there, wide awake, going back over everything — every little thing I think I did wrong, every tone of voice, every task I didn’t finish, every way I feel like I’ve let someone down. It’s like my brain waits until the lights are off and then starts presenting evidence against me. I’ll be exhausted, but I can’t shut it off.\n\nAnd then other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and I’m already tired the second I open my eyes. I’ll stay in bed longer because I’m so wiped out, but it doesn’t help. I don’t get up feeling rested. I used to run, or at least feel like I could function in the morning, and now I just feel depleted before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling really down or depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: Yes… this didn’t just start recently. I mean, this past month has felt like everything crossed some kind of line into unbearable, but the depression itself has been hanging over me for about 11 months now.\n\nAt first I kept trying to tell myself it was just stress, or a rough patch, or that I’d bounce back once things settled down. But it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in the background for almost a year, and lately it’s like the volume got turned way up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like this before the one that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… not in a way that I can clearly point to and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.”\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, definitely — times where I was worn down or sad or overwhelmed — but I don’t know if I ever had something that felt like this, this sustained and heavy. It’s hard because when I look back, I start second-guessing myself, like maybe I minimized things at the time or maybe I just pushed through because I had to. I’m not great at stopping and labeling what’s happening while it’s happening.\n\nBut as far as an episode like this one, before these last 11 months… I can’t say for sure. Nothing is standing out to me as clearly the same.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. That’s actually one of the things that scares me the most, because I used to have things that felt like mine — like they connected me back to myself.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera in… I don’t even know how long. And I used to love that. Not in a professional way, just noticing light or animals or little things outside and wanting to capture them. Now I’ll see the camera sitting there and it’s like it belongs to someone else.\n\nSame with the bird feeders. I used to check them all the time and actually care what birds were coming in, whether they needed refilling, what was showing up that day. Now I might notice they’re there, but there’s no real interest behind it.\n\nAnd the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs tending, what’s about to bloom. I used to look forward to that. Now I just don’t care, and I hate saying that because those things used to be how I felt like myself. So no, I’m not really looking forward to much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received treatment for depression before the episode that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not specifically for depression, before this. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors here and there about stress or feeling run down, things like that, but I wouldn’t say I was ever really in treatment for depression.\n\nAnd part of that is probably because I didn’t name it that way. I tend to just keep functioning until I can’t, and I think I told myself for a long time that if I was still going to work and doing what had to be done, then it didn’t “count” somehow. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s how I handled it.\n\nSo before this episode started 11 months ago, no, I don’t think I had any real treatment for depression.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of depression in your lifetime?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I think… maybe? But I’m not sure I can say that with confidence.\n\nLooking back, there have definitely been times in my life where I felt low or exhausted or kind of emotionally flattened, but I don’t know if those were “episodes” in the way you mean. I didn’t really track it like that, and I don’t think I understood myself that way at the time. I would just tell myself I was stressed, or tired, or that work was taking too much out of me, and then I’d keep going.\n\nThis current one is the one that feels obvious to me, because it’s been so hard to ignore. Before that, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing great, but they’re blurry. I can’t really separate out whether they were true depressions or just life being hard.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know. I can’t clearly say I’ve had more than one, but I also can’t completely rule it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant improvement in your depression symptoms since they started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wish I could say there were stretches where it lifted in a meaningful way, but it’s mostly felt like this has just been hanging over me for about 11 months.\n\nThere may have been individual hours or maybe a day where I could get through things a little more smoothly, but I wouldn’t call that significant improvement. I’m still doing things because they need to be done, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been really bad. I used to be able to sleep and at least feel like I’d had some kind of reset, even if I was tired, but now it doesn’t feel like sleep is doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nA lot of nights I just lie there, wide awake, going back over everything — every little thing I think I did wrong, every tone of voice, every task I didn’t finish, every way I feel like I’ve let someone down. It’s like my brain waits until the lights are off and then starts presenting evidence against me. I’ll be exhausted, but I can’t shut it off.\n\nAnd then other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and I’m already tired the second I open my eyes. I’ll stay in bed longer because I’m so wiped out, but it doesn’t help. I don’t get up feeling rested. I used to run, or at least feel like I could function in the morning, and now I just feel depleted before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling really down or depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: Yes… this didn’t just start recently. I mean, this past month has felt like everything crossed some kind of line into unbearable, but the depression itself has been hanging over me for about 11 months now.\n\nAt first I kept trying to tell myself it was just stress, or a rough patch, or that I’d bounce back once things settled down. But it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in the background for almost a year, and lately it’s like the volume got turned way up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like this before the one that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… not in a way that I can clearly point to and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.”\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, definitely — times where I was worn down or sad or overwhelmed — but I don’t know if I ever had something that felt like this, this sustained and heavy. It’s hard because when I look back, I start second-guessing myself, like maybe I minimized things at the time or maybe I just pushed through because I had to. I’m not great at stopping and labeling what’s happening while it’s happening.\n\nBut as far as an episode like this one, before these last 11 months… I can’t say for sure. Nothing is standing out to me as clearly the same.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. That’s actually one of the things that scares me the most, because I used to have things that felt like mine — like they connected me back to myself.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera in… I don’t even know how long. And I used to love that. Not in a professional way, just noticing light or animals or little things outside and wanting to capture them. Now I’ll see the camera sitting there and it’s like it belongs to someone else.\n\nSame with the bird feeders. I used to check them all the time and actually care what birds were coming in, whether they needed refilling, what was showing up that day. Now I might notice they’re there, but there’s no real interest behind it.\n\nAnd the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs tending, what’s about to bloom. I used to look forward to that. Now I just don’t care, and I hate saying that because those things used to be how I felt like myself. So no, I’m not really looking forward to much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received treatment for depression before the episode that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not specifically for depression, before this. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors here and there about stress or feeling run down, things like that, but I wouldn’t say I was ever really in treatment for depression.\n\nAnd part of that is probably because I didn’t name it that way. I tend to just keep functioning until I can’t, and I think I told myself for a long time that if I was still going to work and doing what had to be done, then it didn’t “count” somehow. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s how I handled it.\n\nSo before this episode started 11 months ago, no, I don’t think I had any real treatment for depression.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of depression in your lifetime?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I think… maybe? But I’m not sure I can say that with confidence.\n\nLooking back, there have definitely been times in my life where I felt low or exhausted or kind of emotionally flattened, but I don’t know if those were “episodes” in the way you mean. I didn’t really track it like that, and I don’t think I understood myself that way at the time. I would just tell myself I was stressed, or tired, or that work was taking too much out of me, and then I’d keep going.\n\nThis current one is the one that feels obvious to me, because it’s been so hard to ignore. Before that, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing great, but they’re blurry. I can’t really separate out whether they were true depressions or just life being hard.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know. I can’t clearly say I’ve had more than one, but I also can’t completely rule it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant improvement in your depression symptoms since they started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wish I could say there were stretches where it lifted in a meaningful way, but it’s mostly felt like this has just been hanging over me for about 11 months.\n\nThere may have been individual hours or maybe a day where I could get through things a little more smoothly, but I wouldn’t call that significant improvement. It never felt like I got myself back, if that makes sense. It was more like the heaviness was still there, just maybe pushed slightly to the side for a moment.\n\nAnd then this past month has made everything feel unbearable, so it’s hard to even remember those smaller variations clearly. But the depression itself… it’s been there the whole time, like a weather system I can’t get out from under.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I wish I had a clearer answer, but when I try to look back before these last 11 months, it’s hard for me to sort it out that neatly.\n\nThere were definitely times when I was functioning better, and probably times when I would have said I was “fine” if someone had asked me. But whether I was completely free of depressive symptoms for two full months… I don’t know. I don’t think I paid attention to it that way. I’m not even sure I would have recognized some of it as depression at the time. I might have just called it being tired, or overwhelmed, or having a rough stretch.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say yes. It’s possible there were periods where things were better for that long, but “completely free” feels like a pretty strong way to put it, and I don’t know that I can confidently say that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely better from depression for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of already answered this, but… no, I can’t say that I ever clearly felt completely better for two months. Not in a way I can point to and say, yes, I was definitely free of it.\n\nThere were probably times before this where I was more functional, where I was getting through work and daily things and maybe not thinking of myself as depressed. It really started around this whole crisis with taking in our relative, and since then everything has felt so up and down and overwhelming.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself it’s just stress and that once things settle down I’ll be fine, but for about a month now it’s been like my mind goes to those places when I’m worn out. Usually I’m thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs pruning, what might bloom soon, and right now I just… don’t care. It feels awful to say that, because those are not just hobbies to me, they’re the things that used to make me feel grounded and like myself. So yes, I’ve definitely felt less interested, and it’s frightening how flat it all feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… constantly. And it’s not always over something that, rationally, I know should carry that much weight, but in my head it becomes this whole indictment of who I am.\n\nLike with everything that’s changed at home, I keep catching myself feeling resentful about the disruption, and then immediately I’m ashamed of that. I’ll think, what kind of person resents this? What kind of person gets irritated when people need help? If I had an impatient look on my face, or if my voice came out sharper than I meant it to, I’ll go over it again and again later. I’ll think, see, that’s who you really are. That proves it. My husband and the family would be better off without having to deal with me and whatever is wrong with me.\n\nI know if someone else told me the same story, I probably wouldn’t judge them that harshly. But when it’s me, it feels like evidence. Like every little thing I do wrong confirms I’m just failing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yes. My energy feels like it’s just… gone. I don’t know how else to say it. Everything takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, even just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s not a long distance, and normally I wouldn’t even think about it — I’d just be moving from one thing to the next — but now I notice every step. I feel heavy, like my body is resisting me.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m completely spent. I can barely make dinner, and even helping with the evening care routine feels like this huge mountain I have to climb. I’m still doing things because they need to be done, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been really bad. I used to be able to sleep and at least feel like I’d had some kind of reset, even if I was tired, but now it doesn’t feel like sleep is doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nA lot of nights I just lie there, wide awake, going back over everything — every little thing I think I did wrong, every tone of voice, every task I didn’t finish, every way I feel like I’ve let someone down. It’s like my brain waits until the lights are off and then starts presenting evidence against me. I’ll be exhausted, but I can’t shut it off.\n\nAnd then other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and I’m already tired the second I open my eyes. I’ll stay in bed longer because I’m so wiped out, but it doesn’t help. I don’t get up feeling rested. I used to run, or at least feel like I could function in the morning, and now I just feel depleted before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling really down or depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: Yes… this didn’t just start recently. I mean, this past month has felt like everything crossed some kind of line into unbearable, but the depression itself has been hanging over me for about 11 months now.\n\nAt first I kept trying to tell myself it was just stress, or a rough patch, or that I’d bounce back once things settled down. But it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in the background for almost a year, and lately it’s like the volume got turned way up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like this before the one that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… not in a way that I can clearly point to and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.”\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, definitely — times where I was worn down or sad or overwhelmed — but I don’t know if I ever had something that felt like this, this sustained and heavy. It’s hard because when I look back, I start second-guessing myself, like maybe I minimized things at the time or maybe I just pushed through because I had to. I’m not great at stopping and labeling what’s happening while it’s happening.\n\nBut as far as an episode like this one, before these last 11 months… I can’t say for sure. Nothing is standing out to me as clearly the same.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. That’s actually one of the things that scares me the most, because I used to have things that felt like mine — like they connected me back to myself.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera in… I don’t even know how long. And I used to love that. Not in a professional way, just noticing light or animals or little things outside and wanting to capture them. Now I’ll see the camera sitting there and it’s like it belongs to someone else.\n\nSame with the bird feeders. I used to check them all the time and actually care what birds were coming in, whether they needed refilling, what was showing up that day. Now I might notice they’re there, but there’s no real interest behind it.\n\nAnd the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs tending, what’s about to bloom. I used to look forward to that. Now I just don’t care, and I hate saying that because those things used to be how I felt like myself. So no, I’m not really looking forward to much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received treatment for depression before the episode that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not specifically for depression, before this. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors here and there about stress or feeling run down, things like that, but I wouldn’t say I was ever really in treatment for depression.\n\nAnd part of that is probably because I didn’t name it that way. I tend to just keep functioning until I can’t, and I think I told myself for a long time that if I was still going to work and doing what had to be done, then it didn’t “count” somehow. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s how I handled it.\n\nSo before this episode started 11 months ago, no, I don’t think I had any real treatment for depression.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of depression in your lifetime?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I think… maybe? But I’m not sure I can say that with confidence.\n\nLooking back, there have definitely been times in my life where I felt low or exhausted or kind of emotionally flattened, but I don’t know if those were “episodes” in the way you mean. I didn’t really track it like that, and I don’t think I understood myself that way at the time. I would just tell myself I was stressed, or tired, or that work was taking too much out of me, and then I’d keep going.\n\nThis current one is the one that feels obvious to me, because it’s been so hard to ignore. Before that, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing great, but they’re blurry. I can’t really separate out whether they were true depressions or just life being hard.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know. I can’t clearly say I’ve had more than one, but I also can’t completely rule it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant improvement in your depression symptoms since they started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wish I could say there were stretches where it lifted in a meaningful way, but it’s mostly felt like this has just been hanging over me for about 11 months.\n\nThere may have been individual hours or maybe a day where I could get through things a little more smoothly, but I wouldn’t call that significant improvement. It never felt like I got myself back, if that makes sense. It was more like the heaviness was still there, just maybe pushed slightly to the side for a moment.\n\nAnd then this past month has made everything feel unbearable, so it’s hard to even remember those smaller variations clearly. But the depression itself… it’s been there the whole time, like a weather system I can’t get out from under.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I wish I had a clearer answer, but when I try to look back before these last 11 months, it’s hard for me to sort it out that neatly.\n\nThere were definitely times when I was functioning better, and probably times when I would have said I was “fine” if someone had asked me. But whether I was completely free of depressive symptoms for two full months… I don’t know. I don’t think I paid attention to it that way. I’m not even sure I would have recognized some of it as depression at the time. I might have just called it being tired, or overwhelmed, or having a rough stretch.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say yes. It’s possible there were periods where things were better for that long, but “completely free” feels like a pretty strong way to put it, and I don’t know that I can confidently say that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely better from depression for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of already answered this, but… no, I can’t say that I ever clearly felt completely better for two months. Not in a way I can point to and say, yes, I was definitely free of it.\n\nThere were probably times before this where I was more functional, where I was getting through work and daily things and maybe not thinking of myself as depressed. But “completely better” feels hard to say. I don’t think I tracked my mood that carefully, and I’m not sure I would’ve noticed if something was still there in the background.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know. I don’t have a clear memory of a solid two-month stretch where I felt fully well.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your depressive symptoms have ever completely gone away for a while before coming back?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a memory of it going away in some clear, complete way and then later coming back, like a switch turning off and on again.\n\nI think there have been times where I was doing better, or where I was busy enough that I wasn’t focused on it, or I could function and tell myself things were okay. But when you say “completely gone away,” I get stuck, because I’m not sure I can identify that. It’s possible I just didn’t notice it as much, or I explained it away as stress or being tired.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to say yes, because that feels too certain. What I can say is I’ve had periods that felt less bad than others, but I can’t really point to a time where I’m confident the depressive symptoms were totally gone for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m probably circling around the same answer, but I don’t think I can honestly say yes to that.\n\nBefore this current episode, I had times where I was more okay on the outside, I guess — like I could get through days, do what I needed to do, maybe not think about it every minute. But when you ask whether I felt completely free of depressive symptoms for two months, that feels too exact for what I remember.\n\nI don’t have a clear stretch I can point to and say, “That was two months where it was totally gone.” Maybe there were better periods, but I don’t know if they were symptom-free or just less noticeable. So I’d say I’m not sure, and I wouldn’t want to claim that I was completely free of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not that I have some big speech prepared or anything, it’s more like this quiet thought that shows up when I’m exhausted.\n\nAnd sometimes, when I’m driving home from the clinic after a long day, I’ll catch myself thinking… what if I just didn’t turn the wheel? Like, what would happen if I just let the car keep going. It’s not every second, but it’s been long enough and intense enough that I finally thought, okay, this isn’t just a bad day anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing, like have you been feeling less interested in hobbies or activities that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes. Usually I’m thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs pruning, what might bloom soon, and right now I just… don’t care. It feels awful to say that, because those are not just hobbies to me, they’re the things that used to make me feel grounded and like myself. I’ll think, what kind of person resents this? What kind of person gets irritated when people need help? If I had an impatient look on my face, or if my voice came out sharper than I meant it to, I’ll go over it again and again later. I’ll think, see, that’s who you really are. That proves it. My husband and the family would be better off without having to deal with me and whatever is wrong with me.\n\nI know if someone else told me the same story, I probably wouldn’t judge them that harshly. But when it’s me, it feels like evidence. Like every little thing I do wrong confirms I’m just failing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yes. My energy feels like it’s just… gone. I don’t know how else to say it. Everything takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, even just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. I feel heavy, like my body is resisting me.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m completely spent. I can barely make dinner, and even helping with the evening care routine feels like this huge mountain I have to climb. I’m still doing things because they need to be done, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been really bad. I used to be able to sleep and at least feel like I’d had some kind of reset, even if I was tired, but now it doesn’t feel like sleep is doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nA lot of nights I just lie there, wide awake, going back over everything — every little thing I think I did wrong, every tone of voice, every task I didn’t finish, every way I feel like I’ve let someone down. It’s like my brain waits until the lights are off and then starts presenting evidence against me. I’ll be exhausted, but I can’t shut it off.\n\nAnd then other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and I’m already tired the second I open my eyes. I’ll stay in bed longer because I’m so wiped out, but it doesn’t help. I don’t get up feeling rested. I used to run, or at least feel like I could function in the morning, and now I just feel depleted before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling really down or depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: Yes… this didn’t just start recently. I mean, this past month has felt like everything crossed some kind of line into unbearable, but the depression itself has been hanging over me for about 11 months now.\n\nAt first I kept trying to tell myself it was just stress, or a rough patch, or that I’d bounce back once things settled down. But it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in the background for almost a year, and lately it’s like the volume got turned way up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like this before the one that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… not in a way that I can clearly point to and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.”\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, definitely — times where I was worn down or sad or overwhelmed — but I don’t know if I ever had something that felt like this, this sustained and heavy. It’s hard because when I look back, I start second-guessing myself, like maybe I minimized things at the time or maybe I just pushed through because I had to. I’m not great at stopping and labeling what’s happening while it’s happening.\n\nBut as far as an episode like this one, before these last 11 months… I can’t say for sure. Nothing is standing out to me as clearly the same.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. That’s actually one of the things that scares me the most, because I used to have things that felt like mine — like they connected me back to myself.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera in… I don’t even know how long. And I used to love that. Not in a professional way, just noticing light or animals or little things outside and wanting to capture them. Now I’ll see the camera sitting there and it’s like it belongs to someone else.\n\nSame with the bird feeders. I used to check them all the time and actually care what birds were coming in, whether they needed refilling, what was showing up that day. Now I might notice they’re there, but there’s no real interest behind it.\n\nAnd the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs tending, what’s about to bloom. I used to look forward to that. Now I just don’t care, and I hate saying that because those things used to be how I felt like myself. So no, I’m not really looking forward to much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received treatment for depression before the episode that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not specifically for depression, before this. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors here and there about stress or feeling run down, things like that, but I wouldn’t say I was ever really in treatment for depression.\n\nAnd part of that is probably because I didn’t name it that way. I tend to just keep functioning until I can’t, and I think I told myself for a long time that if I was still going to work and doing what had to be done, then it didn’t “count” somehow. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s how I handled it.\n\nSo before this episode started 11 months ago, no, I don’t think I had any real treatment for depression.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of depression in your lifetime?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I think… maybe? But I’m not sure I can say that with confidence.\n\nLooking back, there have definitely been times in my life where I felt low or exhausted or kind of emotionally flattened, but I don’t know if those were “episodes” in the way you mean. I didn’t really track it like that, and I don’t think I understood myself that way at the time. I would just tell myself I was stressed, or tired, or that work was taking too much out of me, and then I’d keep going.\n\nThis current one is the one that feels obvious to me, because it’s been so hard to ignore. Before that, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing great, but they’re blurry. I can’t really separate out whether they were true depressions or just life being hard.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know. I can’t clearly say I’ve had more than one, but I also can’t completely rule it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant improvement in your depression symptoms since they started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wish I could say there were stretches where it lifted in a meaningful way, but it’s mostly felt like this has just been hanging over me for about 11 months.\n\nThere may have been individual hours or maybe a day where I could get through things a little more smoothly, but I wouldn’t call that significant improvement. It’s possible there were periods where things were better for that long, but “completely free” feels like a pretty strong way to put it, and I don’t know that I can confidently say that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely better from depression for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of already answered this, but… no, I can’t say that I ever clearly felt completely better for two months. It feels awful to say that, because those are not just hobbies to me, they’re the things that used to make me feel grounded and like myself. So yes, I’ve definitely felt less interested, and it’s frightening how flat it all feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… constantly. And it’s not always over something that, rationally, I know should carry that much weight, but in my head it becomes this whole indictment of who I am.\n\nLike with everything that’s changed at home, I keep catching myself feeling resentful about the disruption, and then immediately I’m ashamed of that. I’ll think, what kind of person resents this? What kind of person gets irritated when people need help? And then I just spiral into telling myself I’m terrible, selfish, cold… all of it.\n\nAnd I replay things, too. If I had an impatient look on my face, or if my voice came out sharper than I meant it to, I’ll go over it again and again later. I’ll think, see, that’s who you really are. That proves it. My husband and the family would be better off without having to deal with me and whatever is wrong with me.\n\nI know if someone else told me the same story, I probably wouldn’t judge them that harshly. But when it’s me, it feels like evidence. Like every little thing I do wrong confirms I’m just failing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yes. My energy feels like it’s just… gone. I don’t know how else to say it. Everything takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, even just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s not a long distance, and normally I wouldn’t even think about it — I’d just be moving from one thing to the next — but now I notice every step. I feel heavy, like my body is resisting me.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m completely spent. I can barely make dinner, and even helping with the evening care routine feels like this huge mountain I have to climb. I’m still doing things because they need to be done, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been really bad. I used to be able to sleep and at least feel like I’d had some kind of reset, even if I was tired, but now it doesn’t feel like sleep is doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nA lot of nights I just lie there, wide awake, going back over everything — every little thing I think I did wrong, every tone of voice, every task I didn’t finish, every way I feel like I’ve let someone down. It’s like my brain waits until the lights are off and then starts presenting evidence against me. I’ll be exhausted, but I can’t shut it off.\n\nAnd then other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and I’m already tired the second I open my eyes. I’ll stay in bed longer because I’m so wiped out, but it doesn’t help. I don’t get up feeling rested. I used to run, or at least feel like I could function in the morning, and now I just feel depleted before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling really down or depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: Yes… this didn’t just start recently. I mean, this past month has felt like everything crossed some kind of line into unbearable, but the depression itself has been hanging over me for about 11 months now.\n\nAt first I kept trying to tell myself it was just stress, or a rough patch, or that I’d bounce back once things settled down. But it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in the background for almost a year, and lately it’s like the volume got turned way up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like this before the one that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… not in a way that I can clearly point to and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.”\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, definitely — times where I was worn down or sad or overwhelmed — but I don’t know if I ever had something that felt like this, this sustained and heavy. It’s hard because when I look back, I start second-guessing myself, like maybe I minimized things at the time or maybe I just pushed through because I had to. I’m not great at stopping and labeling what’s happening while it’s happening.\n\nBut as far as an episode like this one, before these last 11 months… I can’t say for sure. Nothing is standing out to me as clearly the same.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. That’s actually one of the things that scares me the most, because I used to have things that felt like mine — like they connected me back to myself.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera in… I don’t even know how long. And I used to love that. Not in a professional way, just noticing light or animals or little things outside and wanting to capture them. Now I’ll see the camera sitting there and it’s like it belongs to someone else.\n\nSame with the bird feeders. I used to check them all the time and actually care what birds were coming in, whether they needed refilling, what was showing up that day. Now I might notice they’re there, but there’s no real interest behind it.\n\nAnd the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs tending, what’s about to bloom. I used to look forward to that. Now I just don’t care, and I hate saying that because those things used to be how I felt like myself. So no, I’m not really looking forward to much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received treatment for depression before the episode that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not specifically for depression, before this. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors here and there about stress or feeling run down, things like that, but I wouldn’t say I was ever really in treatment for depression.\n\nAnd part of that is probably because I didn’t name it that way. I tend to just keep functioning until I can’t, and I think I told myself for a long time that if I was still going to work and doing what had to be done, then it didn’t “count” somehow. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s how I handled it.\n\nSo before this episode started 11 months ago, no, I don’t think I had any real treatment for depression.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of depression in your lifetime?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I think… maybe? But I’m not sure I can say that with confidence.\n\nLooking back, there have definitely been times in my life where I felt low or exhausted or kind of emotionally flattened, but I don’t know if those were “episodes” in the way you mean. I didn’t really track it like that, and I don’t think I understood myself that way at the time. I would just tell myself I was stressed, or tired, or that work was taking too much out of me, and then I’d keep going.\n\nThis current one is the one that feels obvious to me, because it’s been so hard to ignore. Before that, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing great, but they’re blurry. I can’t really separate out whether they were true depressions or just life being hard.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know. I can’t clearly say I’ve had more than one, but I also can’t completely rule it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant improvement in your depression symptoms since they started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wish I could say there were stretches where it lifted in a meaningful way, but it’s mostly felt like this has just been hanging over me for about 11 months.\n\nThere may have been individual hours or maybe a day where I could get through things a little more smoothly, but I wouldn’t call that significant improvement. It never felt like I got myself back, if that makes sense. It was more like the heaviness was still there, just maybe pushed slightly to the side for a moment.\n\nAnd then this past month has made everything feel unbearable, so it’s hard to even remember those smaller variations clearly. But the depression itself… it’s been there the whole time, like a weather system I can’t get out from under.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I wish I had a clearer answer, but when I try to look back before these last 11 months, it’s hard for me to sort it out that neatly.\n\nThere were definitely times when I was functioning better, and probably times when I would have said I was “fine” if someone had asked me. But whether I was completely free of depressive symptoms for two full months… I don’t know. I don’t think I paid attention to it that way. I’m not even sure I would have recognized some of it as depression at the time. I might have just called it being tired, or overwhelmed, or having a rough stretch.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say yes. It’s possible there were periods where things were better for that long, but “completely free” feels like a pretty strong way to put it, and I don’t know that I can confidently say that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely better from depression for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of already answered this, but… no, I can’t say that I ever clearly felt completely better for two months. Not in a way I can point to and say, yes, I was definitely free of it.\n\nThere were probably times before this where I was more functional, where I was getting through work and daily things and maybe not thinking of myself as depressed. But “completely better” feels hard to say. I don’t think I tracked my mood that carefully, and I’m not sure I would’ve noticed if something was still there in the background.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know. I don’t have a clear memory of a solid two-month stretch where I felt fully well.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your depressive symptoms have ever completely gone away for a while before coming back?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a memory of it going away in some clear, complete way and then later coming back, like a switch turning off and on again.\n\nI think there have been times where I was doing better, or where I was busy enough that I wasn’t focused on it, or I could function and tell myself things were okay. But when you say “completely gone away,” I get stuck, because I’m not sure I can identify that. It’s possible I just didn’t notice it as much, or I explained it away as stress or being tired.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to say yes, because that feels too certain. What I can say is I’ve had periods that felt less bad than others, but I can’t really point to a time where I’m confident the depressive symptoms were totally gone for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m probably circling around the same answer, but I don’t think I can honestly say yes to that.\n\nBefore this current episode, I had times where I was more okay on the outside, I guess — like I could get through days, do what I needed to do, maybe not think about it every minute. So I’d say I’m not sure, and I wouldn’t want to claim that I was completely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before the one that started 11 months ago that were severe enough to require treatment or significantly impact your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, honestly. And then I get frightened because I don’t want to be the kind of person who thinks that, and I don’t want to scare anyone, but it’s been happening enough that I felt like I shouldn’t ignore it anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel while driving?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I think. Not years or anything like that, at least not in this intense way. It really started around this whole crisis with taking in our relative, and since then everything has felt so up and down and overwhelming.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself it’s just stress and that once things settle down I’ll be fine, but for about a month now it’s been like my mind goes to those places when I’m worn out. It’s not every second, but it’s been long enough and intense enough that I finally thought, okay, this isn’t just a bad day anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing, like have you been feeling less interested in hobbies or activities that usually bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. That’s one of the things that scares me, because those things are usually how I know I’m still… me.\n\nI have a camera I used to carry around all the time, even just in the yard or on walks, and I haven’t touched it. Not even picked it up to check the battery or look through old photos. And the bird feeders — normally I’m the person who notices who’s coming in, what birds are out there, whether the feeders need filling, all of that. Lately I’ll look out the window and it’s like I’m seeing it but not really caring. I might think, oh, I should refill that, and then I just don’t.\n\nSame with the garden. Usually I’m thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs pruning, what might bloom soon, and right now I just… don’t care. It feels awful to say that, because those are not just hobbies to me, they’re the things that used to make me feel grounded and like myself. So yes, I’ve definitely felt less interested, and it’s frightening how flat it all feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… constantly. And it’s not always over something that, rationally, I know should carry that much weight, but in my head it becomes this whole indictment of who I am.\n\nLike with everything that’s changed at home, I keep catching myself feeling resentful about the disruption, and then immediately I’m ashamed of that. I’ll think, what kind of person resents this? What kind of person gets irritated when people need help? And then I just spiral into telling myself I’m terrible, selfish, cold… all of it.\n\nAnd I replay things, too. If I had an impatient look on my face, or if my voice came out sharper than I meant it to, I’ll go over it again and again later. I’ll think, see, that’s who you really are. That proves it. My husband and the family would be better off without having to deal with me and whatever is wrong with me.\n\nI know if someone else told me the same story, I probably wouldn’t judge them that harshly. But when it’s me, it feels like evidence. Like every little thing I do wrong confirms I’m just failing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you move compared to before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yes. My energy feels like it’s just… gone. I don’t know how else to say it. Everything takes so much more effort than it used to.\n\nAt work, even just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches feels like I’m dragging myself through mud. It’s not a long distance, and normally I wouldn’t even think about it — I’d just be moving from one thing to the next — but now I notice every step. I feel heavy, like my body is resisting me.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m completely spent. I can barely make dinner, and even helping with the evening care routine feels like this huge mountain I have to climb. I’m still doing things because they need to be done, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement all day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been really bad. I used to be able to sleep and at least feel like I’d had some kind of reset, even if I was tired, but now it doesn’t feel like sleep is doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nA lot of nights I just lie there, wide awake, going back over everything — every little thing I think I did wrong, every tone of voice, every task I didn’t finish, every way I feel like I’ve let someone down. It’s like my brain waits until the lights are off and then starts presenting evidence against me. I’ll be exhausted, but I can’t shut it off.\n\nAnd then other times I do fall asleep, but I wake up before dawn, way too early, and I’m already tired the second I open my eyes. I’ll stay in bed longer because I’m so wiped out, but it doesn’t help. I don’t get up feeling rested. I used to run, or at least feel like I could function in the morning, and now I just feel depleted before the day has even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling really down or depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: Yes… this didn’t just start recently. I mean, this past month has felt like everything crossed some kind of line into unbearable, but the depression itself has been hanging over me for about 11 months now.\n\nAt first I kept trying to tell myself it was just stress, or a rough patch, or that I’d bounce back once things settled down. But it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in the background for almost a year, and lately it’s like the volume got turned way up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode like this before the one that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… not in a way that I can clearly point to and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.”\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, definitely — times where I was worn down or sad or overwhelmed — but I don’t know if I ever had something that felt like this, this sustained and heavy. It’s hard because when I look back, I start second-guessing myself, like maybe I minimized things at the time or maybe I just pushed through because I had to. I’m not great at stopping and labeling what’s happening while it’s happening.\n\nBut as far as an episode like this one, before these last 11 months… I can’t say for sure. Nothing is standing out to me as clearly the same.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. That’s actually one of the things that scares me the most, because I used to have things that felt like mine — like they connected me back to myself.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera in… I don’t even know how long. And I used to love that. Not in a professional way, just noticing light or animals or little things outside and wanting to capture them. Now I’ll see the camera sitting there and it’s like it belongs to someone else.\n\nSame with the bird feeders. I used to check them all the time and actually care what birds were coming in, whether they needed refilling, what was showing up that day. Now I might notice they’re there, but there’s no real interest behind it.\n\nAnd the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead, what’s coming up next, what needs tending, what’s about to bloom. I used to look forward to that. Now I just don’t care, and I hate saying that because those things used to be how I felt like myself. So no, I’m not really looking forward to much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received treatment for depression before the episode that started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not specifically for depression, before this. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors here and there about stress or feeling run down, things like that, but I wouldn’t say I was ever really in treatment for depression.\n\nAnd part of that is probably because I didn’t name it that way. I tend to just keep functioning until I can’t, and I think I told myself for a long time that if I was still going to work and doing what had to be done, then it didn’t “count” somehow. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s how I handled it.\n\nSo before this episode started 11 months ago, no, I don’t think I had any real treatment for depression.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of depression in your lifetime?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I think… maybe? But I’m not sure I can say that with confidence.\n\nLooking back, there have definitely been times in my life where I felt low or exhausted or kind of emotionally flattened, but I don’t know if those were “episodes” in the way you mean. I didn’t really track it like that, and I don’t think I understood myself that way at the time. I would just tell myself I was stressed, or tired, or that work was taking too much out of me, and then I’d keep going.\n\nThis current one is the one that feels obvious to me, because it’s been so hard to ignore. Before that, I can remember periods where I wasn’t doing great, but they’re blurry. I can’t really separate out whether they were true depressions or just life being hard.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know. I can’t clearly say I’ve had more than one, but I also can’t completely rule it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of significant improvement in your depression symptoms since they started 11 months ago?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wish I could say there were stretches where it lifted in a meaningful way, but it’s mostly felt like this has just been hanging over me for about 11 months.\n\nThere may have been individual hours or maybe a day where I could get through things a little more smoothly, but I wouldn’t call that significant improvement. It never felt like I got myself back, if that makes sense. It was more like the heaviness was still there, just maybe pushed slightly to the side for a moment.\n\nAnd then this past month has made everything feel unbearable, so it’s hard to even remember those smaller variations clearly. But the depression itself… it’s been there the whole time, like a weather system I can’t get out from under.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I wish I had a clearer answer, but when I try to look back before these last 11 months, it’s hard for me to sort it out that neatly.\n\nThere were definitely times when I was functioning better, and probably times when I would have said I was “fine” if someone had asked me. But whether I was completely free of depressive symptoms for two full months… I don’t know. I don’t think I paid attention to it that way. I’m not even sure I would have recognized some of it as depression at the time. I might have just called it being tired, or overwhelmed, or having a rough stretch.\n\nSo I can’t honestly say yes. It’s possible there were periods where things were better for that long, but “completely free” feels like a pretty strong way to put it, and I don’t know that I can confidently say that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt completely better from depression for at least two months before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of already answered this, but… no, I can’t say that I ever clearly felt completely better for two months. Not in a way I can point to and say, yes, I was definitely free of it.\n\nThere were probably times before this where I was more functional, where I was getting through work and daily things and maybe not thinking of myself as depressed. But “completely better” feels hard to say. I don’t think I tracked my mood that carefully, and I’m not sure I would’ve noticed if something was still there in the background.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I don’t know. I don’t have a clear memory of a solid two-month stretch where I felt fully well.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your depressive symptoms have ever completely gone away for a while before coming back?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t have a memory of it going away in some clear, complete way and then later coming back, like a switch turning off and on again.\n\nI think there have been times where I was doing better, or where I was busy enough that I wasn’t focused on it, or I could function and tell myself things were okay. But when you say “completely gone away,” I get stuck, because I’m not sure I can identify that. It’s possible I just didn’t notice it as much, or I explained it away as stress or being tired.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to say yes, because that feels too certain. What I can say is I’ve had periods that felt less bad than others, but I can’t really point to a time where I’m confident the depressive symptoms were totally gone for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m probably circling around the same answer, but I don’t think I can honestly say yes to that.\n\nBefore this current episode, I had times where I was more okay on the outside, I guess — like I could get through days, do what I needed to do, maybe not think about it every minute. But when you ask whether I felt completely free of depressive symptoms for two months, that feels too exact for what I remember.\n\nI don’t have a clear stretch I can point to and say, “That was two months where it was totally gone.” Maybe there were better periods, but I don’t know if they were symptom-free or just less noticeable. So I’d say I’m not sure, and I wouldn’t want to claim that I was completely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before the one that started 11 months ago that were severe enough to require treatment or significantly impact your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, honestly. I don’t have a clean memory of an earlier “episode” where I can say, yes, that was definitely depression and it was bad enough that I needed treatment.\n\nThere were probably times in my life where I wasn’t doing great and daily life felt harder than it should have, but I don’t know if I would call those full episodes. I don’t want to overstate it or make it sound more definite than it is. Before the one that started about 11 months ago, I can’t clearly point to another period that I know required treatment or clearly disrupted my life in that same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any previous episodes of depression that were similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say I’ve had anything before that was really similar to this.\n\nI’ve had times where I felt low or not like myself, and I can remember periods where things felt harder than they probably should have, but I’m not sure I’d put them in the same category as what’s been going on now. This feels more definite to me, or at least it’s the one I can recognize most clearly when you ask about it.\n\nI’m trying not to answer too strongly just because I assume there must have been earlier times. I can sort of look back and wonder, “Was that depression too?” but I don’t have a clear previous episode in my mind that I’d say was similar to this one. So… I’d say not that I can clearly identify.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t have a memory of it going away in some clear, complete way and then later coming back, like a switch turning off and on again.\n\nI think there have been times where I was doing better, or where I was busy enough that I wasn’t focused on it, or I could function and tell myself things were okay. But when you say “completely gone away,” I get stuck, because I’m not sure I can identify that. It’s possible I just didn’t notice it as much, or I explained it away as stress or being tired.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to say yes, because that feels too certain. What I can say is I’ve had periods that felt less bad than others, but I can’t really point to a time where I’m confident the depressive symptoms were totally gone for a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know I’m probably circling around the same answer, but I don’t think I can honestly say yes to that.\n\nBefore this current episode, I had times where I was more okay on the outside, I guess — like I could get through days, do what I needed to do, maybe not think about it every minute. But when you ask whether I felt completely free of depressive symptoms for two months, that feels too exact for what I remember.\n\nI don’t have a clear stretch I can point to and say, “That was two months where it was totally gone.” Maybe there were better periods, but I don’t know if they were symptom-free or just less noticeable. So I’d say I’m not sure, and I wouldn’t want to claim that I was completely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before the one that started 11 months ago that were severe enough to require treatment or significantly impact your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to separate that out, honestly. I don’t have a clean memory of an earlier “episode” where I can say, yes, that was definitely depression and it was bad enough that I needed treatment.\n\nThere were probably times in my life where I wasn’t doing great and daily life felt harder than it should have, but I don’t know if I would call those full episodes. I don’t want to overstate it or make it sound more definite than it is. Before the one that started about 11 months ago, I can’t clearly point to another period that I know required treatment or clearly disrupted my life in that same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any previous episodes of depression that were similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say I’ve had anything before that was really similar to this.\n\nI’ve had times where I felt low or not like myself, and I can remember periods where things felt harder than they probably should have, but I’m not sure I’d put them in the same category as what’s been going on now. This feels more definite to me, or at least it’s the one I can recognize most clearly when you ask about it.\n\nI’m trying not to answer too strongly just because I assume there must have been earlier times. I can sort of look back and wonder, “Was that depression too?” but I don’t have a clear previous episode in my mind that I’d say was similar to this one. So… I’d say not that I can clearly identify."
